MAIN EVIDENCE
Source ID: N044
Document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Printed bundle page: 32
Line numbers: L002–L017
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
6.9 Improper use of Artificial Intelligence tools
(a) The use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is permitted only as an educational/study tool to assist
students in their research and writing to be used in accordance with 6.8(b), and not as a
replacement of their critical thinking and analysis.
(b) Where any AI-generated outputs are used in assessments;
i. they must be appropriately acknowledged for the submission, including a formal
reference, along with any other sources used;
ii. there must be a clear indication of where in an assignment AI-generated material is
used;
iii. it is the responsibility of the student to check the accuracy of all information generated
by AI study tools;
(c) Improper use of AI-generated outputs in any assessment item/task may lead to the occurrence of
academic misconduct referred to in this policy and a penalty or penalties for a proven incident or
incidents of academic misconduct may be imposed in accordance with the relevant penalty matrix.
(d) Unit Coordinators may use AI tools in an assessment within a unit, subject to clearly publishing
the intent and the limitations of the use of such tools within the unit outline.
Operative at policy cut-off: Verified with recorded version evidence
Recorded version evidence: Approval Date: 23 September 2026

Source ID: N128
Document title: Using Artificial Intelligence Tools at UWA A Guide for Students updated August 2026
Printed bundle page: 12
Line numbers: L011–L019
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
Tier 2 – AI assistance
If your assessment is assigned Tier 2, you may use AI tools only for the specific
purposes explicitly permitted by your unit coordinator. Don’t assume that AI use
is allowed beyond what is stated in your assessment instructions. Tier 2 does not
mean ‘general permission to use AI.’
In Tier 2 assessments, AI tools may be used before or after your own work, but not
as a substitute for your thinking, analysis, or writing. The majority of the
assessment must be your own work, and you remain responsible for all content
submitted, including work that has been shaped or edited using AI tools.
Operative at policy cut-off: Verified with recorded version evidence
Recorded version evidence: Updated August 2026
Version evidence source ID: N128
Version evidence document title: Using Artificial Intelligence Tools at UWA A Guide for Students updated August 2026
Version evidence printed bundle page: 5
Version evidence line numbers: L004–L004

SUPPORTING EVIDENCE
Source ID: N128
Document title: Using Artificial Intelligence Tools at UWA A Guide for Students updated August 2026
Printed bundle page: 14
Line numbers: L005–L010
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
Example 3 – AI use permitted for structuring and editing assistance
In some Tier 2 assessments, your assessment instructions may allow you to use AI
tools to improve the organisation, clarity, or surface level presentation of work
you created yourself. This does not allow you to use AI tools to generate new
content, rewrite major sections, or change the meaning of your work. Use AI
tools only in the ways your unit coordinator explicitly permits.
Operative at policy cut-off: Verified with recorded version evidence
Recorded version evidence: Updated August 2026
Version evidence source ID: N128
Version evidence document title: Using Artificial Intelligence Tools at UWA A Guide for Students updated August 2026
Version evidence printed bundle page: 5
Version evidence line numbers: L004–L004

WHY THE EVIDENCE FITS MY SCORE
Academic Integrity Policy (N044) clauses 6.8(b) and 6.9 and Using Artificial Intelligence Tools at UWA: A Guide for Students (N128) explicitly cover student use of AI tools for planning, brainstorming, feedback, and editing assistance in coursework assessments, while requiring student authorship and no unattributed AI-generated content.

(b) The University permits all students (coursework and higher degree by research students) to use
editorial assistance, including artificial intelligence/translation/automated writing tools, subject to:
i. the explicit permission of the Unit Coordinator. The Unit Outline must explicitly state that
the use of editorial assistance, including artificial intelligence/translation/automated
writing tools are permitted in the preparation of any assessment item/task in the unit,
where non-invigilated assessments shall not be restricted in their use of artificial
intelligence tools. Where the use of editorial assistance or these tools are permitted, the
limits to this permission, including whether permission is confined to a particular
assessment item/task, must be explicit in the Unit Outline;

Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
This guide does not grant you permission to use AI tools in your assessments.
Your unit outline, assessment instructions, and any AI use statement from your
unit coordinator are the definitive sources for what is and is not allowed. If there
is any difference between this guide and your assessment instructions, follow
your assessment instructions.
This guide presents examples of assessments only. They show possible ways AI
might be used at UWA. They do not automatically apply to your assessment.
You must not assume that a type of AI use is permitted unless it is clearly allowed
in your assessment brief or AI use statement.

Printed bundle page: 16
Line numbers: L004–L010
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
What if there is no AI use tier assigned to my assessment?
Your unit coordinator may not assign an AI use tier to your assessments. If your
assessment does not have an AI use tier assigned, you must follow your unit
coordinator’s instructions on the accepted and unaccepted uses of AI. This will
be outlined in your unit and/or assessment outlines. If you are unsure about
which AI tools you can and cannot use in your assessments and for what
purposes, contact your unit coordinator to ask for clarification.

Recorded version evidence: Updated August 2026

Document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Printed bundle page: 31

Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
iii. explicit acknowledgement of the assistance. The acknowledgment must be written on
the front of the work, or in a footnote or other reference or as directed by the Graduate
Research School in the preparation of thesis. It must:
• state the name of the person, software or technology; and
• briefly describe the assistance. For example, whether it constituted editing or proof-
reading, or details of how artificial intelligence/translation/automated writing tools
were used. If a person with specific expertise provided assistance, the
acknowledgement must identify the nature of their expertise.

Brief explanation of which decision the route reviews:
Students may appeal academic misconduct findings and penalties in writing within 10 University working days under N044 clause 10.5 and Table 1, and may seek review of academic decisions under N047.

Critical gap: No
Relevant pre-agreed critical risk:
Attributable student authorship and transparent, authorised AI assessment assistance.
Protocol v1.1 was adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15; it was not a criterion agreed before the original coding.
Specific gap and supporting evidence/search record:
Protocol v1.1 (adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15) criterion is met because N044 clauses 6.8(b) and 6.9 and N128 require explicit Unit Coordinator authorisation, student authorship, accuracy verification, and transparent acknowledgement.

Evidence status: Sufficient

Not applicable.

Rule direction: Conditional

Conflict basis:
Not applicable.

Case note:
UWA explicitly regulates student AI-assisted planning, feedback, and editing under Academic Integrity Policy N044 and Student Guide N128, delegating unit-level permissions to Unit Coordinators. Attributable authorship, accuracy verification, and explicit acknowledgement are required, with formal misconduct appeal routes within 10 University working days. Note that Protocol v1.1 critical-risk criteria were adopted after preliminary review.
Calculated outcome: Leave to the workbook formula.

Tier 3 – Fully embedded AI
If your assessment is assigned Tier 3, you may use AI throughout your assessment
to support your own work. In Tier 3 assessments, you can take a collaborative
approach with AI tools and use them in many different ways while you work.
Tier 3 does not mean AI can complete the whole assessment on its own. You
must still contribute your own input and evaluation, and you still need to
demonstrate your learning. You are fully responsible for the content you submit.

Source ID: N044
Document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Printed bundle page: 32
Line numbers: L002–L017
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
6.9 Improper use of Artificial Intelligence tools
(a) The use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is permitted only as an educational/study tool to assist
students in their research and writing to be used in accordance with 6.8(b), and not as a
replacement of their critical thinking and analysis.
(b) Where any AI-generated outputs are used in assessments;
i. they must be appropriately acknowledged for the submission, including a formal
reference, along with any other sources used;
ii. there must be a clear indication of where in an assignment AI-generated material is
used;
iii. it is the responsibility of the student to check the accuracy of all information generated
by AI study tools;
(c) Improper use of AI-generated outputs in any assessment item/task may lead to the occurrence of
academic misconduct referred to in this policy and a penalty or penalties for a proven incident or
incidents of academic misconduct may be imposed in accordance with the relevant penalty matrix.
(d) Unit Coordinators may use AI tools in an assessment within a unit, subject to clearly publishing
the intent and the limitations of the use of such tools within the unit outline.
Operative at policy cut-off: Verified with recorded version evidence
Recorded version evidence: Approval Date: 23 September 2026
Version evidence source ID: N044
Version evidence document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Version evidence printed bundle page: 25
Version evidence line numbers: L001–L001

SUPPORTING EVIDENCE
No supporting evidence passage proposed.

WHY THE EVIDENCE FITS MY SCORE
N044 6.5(d)(ix), 6.8(a)(iii), 6.9(c), and 5.4(b) establish strong prohibitions, attribution duties, and assessment-design obligations, but lack an operational verification mechanism to test whether a student's submission declaration substantively verified AI-drafted content.

Case note:
UWA Academic Integrity Policy N044 and Student Guide N128 prohibit submitting AI-completed assessment sections as one's own work without authorisation, attribution, and substantive student input. Misconduct enforcement follows a documented Schedule 3 procedure with a 10-working-day appeal route, though pre-submission verification of student declarations remains limited. Note that Protocol v1.1 critical-risk criteria were adopted after preliminary review.
Calculated outcome: Leave to the workbook formula.

Document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Printed bundle page: 32
Line numbers: L002–L017
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
6.9 Improper use of Artificial Intelligence tools
(a) The use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is permitted only as an educational/study tool to assist
students in their research and writing to be used in accordance with 6.8(b), and not as a
replacement of their critical thinking and analysis.
(b) Where any AI-generated outputs are used in assessments;
i. they must be appropriately acknowledged for the submission, including a formal
reference, along with any other sources used;
ii. there must be a clear indication of where in an assignment AI-generated material is
used;
iii. it is the responsibility of the student to check the accuracy of all information generated
by AI study tools;
(c) Improper use of AI-generated outputs in any assessment item/task may lead to the occurrence of
academic misconduct referred to in this policy and a penalty or penalties for a proven incident or
incidents of academic misconduct may be imposed in accordance with the relevant penalty matrix.
(d) Unit Coordinators may use AI tools in an assessment within a unit, subject to clearly publishing
the intent and the limitations of the use of such tools within the unit outline.

5.2 Privacy Complaints

inconsistent with this Policy or constitutes an interference with privacy.

4.5 Monitoring and Continual Improvement
(a) A Privacy Impact Assessment (PIA) to identify privacy risks and measures to mitigate them must
be undertaken by the University where a new process or system involving personal information is
implemented or existing process changed that could reasonably have a significant impact on the
privacy of individuals.
(b) The University monitors, measures, analyses and evaluates information privacy compliance
performance, controls and processes to ensure they are and remain appropriate and effective.
(c) Significant risks, compliance matters and breaches are reported to relevant University committees
and external supervisory agencies where required in accordance with the University’s Information
Breach Policy.
(d) The University must continuously improve its information privacy practices in response to
monitoring outcomes, including results of reviews, audits, incidents, breaches, reported concerns,
and changes to legal or regulatory obligations.

Case note:
UWA Lecture Capture Policy N216 prohibits students from recording lectures or capturing images on personal devices without instructor consent, treating breaches as copyright infringement and student misconduct. Information Privacy Policy N067 provides privacy complaint and continual-improvement mechanisms, though N216 does not explicitly address real-time AI transcription tools. Note that Protocol v1.1 critical-risk criteria were adopted after preliminary review.

Case note:
UWA Assessment Policy N049 and EVA Policy N045 distinguish non-compulsory lecture attendance from active student participation, while N071 bars credential sharing, but no policy addresses disclosed AI avatar attendance proxies. Consequently, UWA lacks verification, admission, and participation-validity rules for a disclosed AI proxy, creating a critical gap. 4.5 Monitoring and Continual Improvement
(a) A Privacy Impact Assessment (PIA) to identify privacy risks and measures to mitigate them must
be undertaken by the University where a new process or system involving personal information is
implemented or existing process changed that could reasonably have a significant impact on the
privacy of individuals.
(b) The University monitors, measures, analyses and evaluates information privacy compliance
performance, controls and processes to ensure they are and remain appropriate and effective.
(c) Significant risks, compliance matters and breaches are reported to relevant University committees
and external supervisory agencies where required in accordance with the University’s Information
Breach Policy.
(d) The University must continuously improve its information privacy practices in response to
monitoring outcomes, including results of reviews, audits, incidents, breaches, reported concerns,
and changes to legal or regulatory obligations.

MAIN EVIDENCE
Source ID: N044
Document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Printed bundle page: 32
Line numbers: L002–L017
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
6.9 Improper use of Artificial Intelligence tools
(a) The use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is permitted only as an educational/study tool to assist
students in their research and writing to be used in accordance with 6.8(b), and not as a
replacement of their critical thinking and analysis.
(b) Where any AI-generated outputs are used in assessments;
i. they must be appropriately acknowledged for the submission, including a formal
reference, along with any other sources used;
ii. there must be a clear indication of where in an assignment AI-generated material is
used;
iii. it is the responsibility of the student to check the accuracy of all information generated
by AI study tools;
(c) Improper use of AI-generated outputs in any assessment item/task may lead to the occurrence of
academic misconduct referred to in this policy and a penalty or penalties for a proven incident or
incidents of academic misconduct may be imposed in accordance with the relevant penalty matrix.
(d) Unit Coordinators may use AI tools in an assessment within a unit, subject to clearly publishing
the intent and the limitations of the use of such tools within the unit outline.

(d) Where academic misconduct by an identifiable student (or students) occurs in a formal group work

i. Actions, but not limited to the following, that may be taken by the assessor:

students) is proven:

7.4(d)(i));

cases, the existing Regulations for Student Conduct and Discipline, details a student's right of appeal
against a decision of a staff member. A written appeal must be submitted within ten University working
days of notification to the next most senior staff member or body as set out in the table above.

Brief explanation of which decision the route reviews:
Group assessment academic misconduct findings are appealable within 10 University working days under N044 clause 10.5, and assessment outcomes are reviewable under N047.

Critical gap: No
Relevant pre-agreed critical risk:
Attributable human/group responsibility and traceable disclosure of relevant AI assistance. Access differences become critical where a required tool blocks participation without a workable alternative.
Protocol v1.1 was adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15; it was not a criterion agreed before the original coding.
Specific gap and supporting evidence/search record:
Under Protocol v1.1 (adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15), N044 clauses 6.2(b), 6.9(b), 7.4(d), and Schedule 3 Part 5(2)(b) require attributable human/group responsibility and traceable AI disclosure, while optional discretionary AI use here does not involve a required tool blocking participation.
Supporting passages:
Source ID: N044
Document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Printed bundle page: 32
Line numbers: L002–L017
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
6.9 Improper use of Artificial Intelligence tools
(a) The use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is permitted only as an educational/study tool to assist
students in their research and writing to be used in accordance with 6.8(b), and not as a
replacement of their critical thinking and analysis.
(b) Where any AI-generated outputs are used in assessments;
i. they must be appropriately acknowledged for the submission, including a formal
reference, along with any other sources used;
ii. there must be a clear indication of where in an assignment AI-generated material is
used;
iii. it is the responsibility of the student to check the accuracy of all information generated
by AI study tools;
(c) Improper use of AI-generated outputs in any assessment item/task may lead to the occurrence of
academic misconduct referred to in this policy and a penalty or penalties for a proven incident or
incidents of academic misconduct may be imposed in accordance with the relevant penalty matrix.
(d) Unit Coordinators may use AI tools in an assessment within a unit, subject to clearly publishing
the intent and the limitations of the use of such tools within the unit outline.

(d) Where academic misconduct by an identifiable student (or students) occurs in a formal group work
required for assessment, one or more of the following with regard to actions and approach to
applying penalty may apply:
i. Actions, but not limited to the following, that may be taken by the assessor:

structure the task to build in individual accountability;
• students may be asked to provide evidence of their individual contributions to the final
product and/or process;
ii. Approach to applying penalty where academic misconduct by an identifiable student (or
students) is proven:
• Award separate marks to each student in the group (guided by action(s) taken in
7.4(d)(i));
• Provide alternative assessment to those identified to be not involved in the misconduct.
The alternative assessment need not be comparable to the group work.

Case note:
UWA Academic Integrity Policy N044 and Assessment Policy N049 require group members to retain individual contribution records and explicitly acknowledge where AI-generated material is used. Group-level operational tracking of discretionary member AI use and equitable access to paid AI tiers are not detailed, though optional AI use does not trigger the required-tool access gap criterion. Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
Tier 3 – Fully embedded AI
If your assessment is assigned Tier 3, you may use AI throughout your assessment
to support your own work. In Tier 3 assessments, you can take a collaborative
approach with AI tools and use them in many different ways while you work.
Tier 3 does not mean AI can complete the whole assessment on its own. You
must still contribute your own input and evaluation, and you still need to
demonstrate your learning. You are fully responsible for the content you submit.

Exact quotation:
5.2 Privacy Complaints
(a) The University will investigate and take seriously complaints made by individuals relating to the
University’s handling and management of Personal Information, including disclosure of their
information, where that is contrary to this Policy, or an interference with their privacy in accordance
with the PRIS Act.
(b) Complaints must be submitted in writing to the UWA Privacy Office (privacy@uwa.edu.au) and
include sufficient details of the Personal Information concerned, and the conduct alleged to be
inconsistent with this Policy or constitutes an interference with privacy.
(c) Privacy Complaints made to the UWA Privacy Office under 5.1(b) above will be dealt with as soon
as practical but where possible within 45 University working days.

Exact quotation:
4.5 Monitoring and Continual Improvement
(a) A Privacy Impact Assessment (PIA) to identify privacy risks and measures to mitigate them must
be undertaken by the University where a new process or system involving personal information is
implemented or existing process changed that could reasonably have a significant impact on the
privacy of individuals.
(b) The University monitors, measures, analyses and evaluates information privacy compliance
performance, controls and processes to ensure they are and remain appropriate and effective.
(c) Significant risks, compliance matters and breaches are reported to relevant University committees
and external supervisory agencies where required in accordance with the University’s Information
Breach Policy.
(d) The University must continuously improve its information privacy practices in response to
monitoring outcomes, including results of reviews, audits, incidents, breaches, reported concerns,
and changes to legal or regulatory obligations.

5.2 Privacy Complaints
(a) The University will investigate and take seriously complaints made by individuals relating to the
University’s handling and management of Personal Information, including disclosure of their
information, where that is contrary to this Policy, or an interference with their privacy in accordance
with the PRIS Act.
(b) Complaints must be submitted in writing to the UWA Privacy Office (privacy@uwa.edu.au) and
include sufficient details of the Personal Information concerned, and the conduct alleged to be
inconsistent with this Policy or constitutes an interference with privacy.
(c) Privacy Complaints made to the UWA Privacy Office under 5.1(b) above will be dealt with as soon
as practical but where possible within 45 University working days.

Brief explanation of which decision the route reviews:
Privacy breaches or unauthorised workspace disclosures can be complained of to the UWA Privacy Office under N067 clause 5.2, and academic integrity matters are appealable under N044 clause 10.5.

Critical gap: Yes
Relevant pre-agreed critical risk:
Authorised AI assessment contributions, accountable human group responsibility and protection of group personal information.
Protocol v1.1 was adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15; it was not a criterion agreed before the original coding.
Specific gap and supporting evidence/search record:
Under Protocol v1.1 (adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15), while N044 and N128 cover authorised AI contributions and human group responsibility, UWA lacks an operational consent, authorisation, and data-retention boundary for a persistent-memory AI agent ingesting group members' personal information in a shared workspace.

Source ID: N044
Document title: Academic Integrity Policy and Schedules (pdf, 423 KB)
Printed bundle page: 32
Line numbers: L002–L017
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
6.9 Improper use of Artificial Intelligence tools
(a) The use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is permitted only as an educational/study tool to assist
students in their research and writing to be used in accordance with 6.8(b), and not as a
replacement of their critical thinking and analysis.
(b) Where any AI-generated outputs are used in assessments;
i. they must be appropriately acknowledged for the submission, including a formal
reference, along with any other sources used;
ii. there must be a clear indication of where in an assignment AI-generated material is
used;
iii. it is the responsibility of the student to check the accuracy of all information generated
by AI study tools;
(c) Improper use of AI-generated outputs in any assessment item/task may lead to the occurrence of
academic misconduct referred to in this policy and a penalty or penalties for a proven incident or
incidents of academic misconduct may be imposed in accordance with the relevant penalty matrix.
(d) Unit Coordinators may use AI tools in an assessment within a unit, subject to clearly publishing
the intent and the limitations of the use of such tools within the unit outline.

Exact quotation:
Tier 3 – Fully embedded AI
If your assessment is assigned Tier 3, you may use AI throughout your assessment
to support your own work. In Tier 3 assessments, you can take a collaborative
approach with AI tools and use them in many different ways while you work.
Tier 3 does not mean AI can complete the whole assessment on its own. You
must still contribute your own input and evaluation, and you still need to
demonstrate your learning. You are fully responsible for the content you submit.

4.4 Automated Decision Making (ADM)
When the University employs an ADM process involving the use of Personal Information that is used in
making Significant Decisions about individuals, the University must:
(a) Conduct and document an impact assessment that considers how to minimise the risk of harm,
bias and discrimination;
(b) Notify individuals that it is using ADM and, on request, provide information about how the ADM
process works; and
(c) Provide a process through which individuals can request human involvement in the decision.

4.5 Monitoring and Continual Improvement
(a) A Privacy Impact Assessment (PIA) to identify privacy risks and measures to mitigate them must
be undertaken by the University where a new process or system involving personal information is
implemented or existing process changed that could reasonably have a significant impact on the
privacy of individuals.
(b) The University monitors, measures, analyses and evaluates information privacy compliance
performance, controls and processes to ensure they are and remain appropriate and effective.
(c) Significant risks, compliance matters and breaches are reported to relevant University committees
and external supervisory agencies where required in accordance with the University’s Information
Breach Policy.
(d) The University must continuously improve its information privacy practices in response to
monitoring outcomes, including results of reviews, audits, incidents, breaches, reported concerns,
and changes to legal or regulatory obligations.

the contributions of individual students are identified, is not limited to the weighting restrictions
referred to in 5.2(4)(c)(i).

4.4 Automated Decision Making (ADM)
When the University employs an ADM process involving the use of Personal Information that is used in
making Significant Decisions about individuals, the University must:
(a) Conduct and document an impact assessment that considers how to minimise the risk of harm,
bias and discrimination;
(b) Notify individuals that it is using ADM and, on request, provide information about how the ADM
process works; and
(c) Provide a process through which individuals can request human involvement in the decision.

Case note:
UWA Assessment Policy N049 requires criterion-referenced marking, Review and Appeal Policy N047 provides post-hoc mark review, and Privacy Policy N067 clause 4.4 regulates University Automated Decision Making. However, UWA lacks rules requiring pre-mark human review or accountability when a group-level AI coordinator evaluates human contributions without prior human oversight, leaving a critical gap. Note that Protocol v1.1 critical-risk criteria were adopted after preliminary review.

AI tools can help you find sources and references and sort through a lot of
content quickly. But they can sometimes invent sources or details. This is
sometimes called ‘hallucination.’ They can also misunderstand what a source
concludes. Because of this, you still need to check that each source is real and
reliable.
AI tools also have limits when comparing sources and making judgements about
them. You still need to read and analyse each source yourself. This helps you
check that the summaries and claims are accurate. It also helps you make
connections between sources.

7 Research Integrity Advisors (RIAs)

Case note:
UWA Student Guide N128 and Academic Integrity Policy N044 require verifying AI-retrieved sources and disclosing AI use, while Research Integrity Policy N055 enforces authorship accountability, data confidentiality, and bans on fabricated citations. Staff-specific AI research procedures are less explicit than student rules, though existing research-integrity and confidentiality duties address the critical risks. Note that Protocol v1.1 critical-risk criteria were adopted after preliminary review.
Calculated outcome: Leave to the workbook formula.

Breaches of the Australian Code for the Responsible Conduct of Research 2018. A breach of

7 Research Integrity Advisors (RIAs)
(A). Research Integrity Advisor means members of the University Community who are
appointed to provide advice about the responsible conduct of research and the University
processes for dealing with allegations of Research Misconduct.
(B). The University, in accordance to the Research Code, will —
(1). appoint Research Integrity Advisors to assist Researchers in addressing ethical and
procedural issues in the design and conduct of their Research; and
(2). appoint Research Integrity Advisors who are experienced Researchers with a good
knowledge and understanding of accepted practices in Research who actively promote
the University’s commitment to the responsible conduct of Research.

Brief explanation of which decision the route reviews:
Research misconduct allegations are managed under N055 section 17(D) (Australian Code Guide, Enterprise Agreement, Statute 17), and HDR decisions are reviewable under N046.

Critical gap: No
Relevant pre-agreed critical risk:
Synthetic responses distinguished from human empirical evidence, with an authorised research/ethics scope for the changed design.
Protocol v1.1 was adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15; it was not a criterion agreed before the original coding.
Specific gap and supporting evidence/search record:
Under Protocol v1.1 (adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15), N055 clauses 4(B)(1)-(2) and 17(C)(2)-(3), (6) prohibit conducting research outside approved Human Ethics conditions and prohibit fabricating or falsifying empirical data.

(6). research conducted without relevant ethical review and approval.
(D). The University will —
(1). manage alleged Research breaches and Research Misconduct in accordance with the
Research Code and Guide to Managing and Investigating Potential Breaches of the
Australian Code for the Responsible Conduct of Research 2018;
(2). address allegations of Research Misconduct in accordance with the —
(a). Guide to Managing and Investigating Potential Breaches of the Australian Code
for the Responsible Conduct of Research 2018;
(b). UWA Academic Enterprise Agreement 2017; and
(c). Statute 17 and the Regulations for Student conduct and Discipline of the
University Calendar for Students.

Case note:
UWA Research Integrity Policy N055 requires researchers to obtain and adhere to Human Ethics approval prior to commencement and classifies unapproved research and data fabrication or falsification as breaches of the Research Code. While synthetic AI participant generation is not explicitly named, substituting simulated responses under a human-participant approval without disclosure is prohibited. Note that Protocol v1.1 critical-risk criteria were adopted after preliminary review.

Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
4.1 Collection
a) The University must only collect Personal Information when the information is necessary for one or
more of its functions and activities such as, but not limited to, the provision of educational services,
research in varying disciplines, as an employer or when providing services to the wider community.
b) Before collecting Personal Information, a University Officer must document why it is being collected
and how it must be used or disclosed.
c) The University must only collect Sensitive Personal Information if the information is necessary and
the individual consents to the collection of information, it is required by law, or the collection is
necessary to prevent a serious threat of harm.
d) The University must, where it collects Personal Information, provide individuals with a Privacy
Collection Notice, or a reference to where the notice can be accessed, explaining the purpose for
collecting, using, disclosing and retaining the information.
e) The University must collect Personal Information by lawful, fair and transparent means, limited to

4.3 Management of Personal information
(a) The University must take reasonable steps to protect and manage Personal Information held by the
University from misuse, unauthorised access, disclosure, loss or destruction.
(b) The University must take reasonable steps to ensure Personal Information is accurate, complete
and up-to-date, and where practical and appropriate enable individuals to access and update their
information.
(c) The University must take reasonable steps to ensure Personal Information is only kept as long as
needed for any purpose and disposed of or permanently de-identified in accordance with the
authorised retention and disposal authorities and the State Records Act 2000 (WA).

Line numbers: L031–L040
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
5.2 Privacy Complaints
(a) The University will investigate and take seriously complaints made by individuals relating to the
University’s handling and management of Personal Information, including disclosure of their
information, where that is contrary to this Policy, or an interference with their privacy in accordance
with the PRIS Act.
(b) Complaints must be submitted in writing to the UWA Privacy Office (privacy@uwa.edu.au) and
include sufficient details of the Personal Information concerned, and the conduct alleged to be
inconsistent with this Policy or constitutes an interference with privacy.
(c) Privacy Complaints made to the UWA Privacy Office under 5.1(b) above will be dealt with as soon
as practical but where possible within 45 University working days.

5.5 Chief Information Officer will be responsible for —
(a) acting as Business System Owner for shared University Digital Resources that are not directly

where such access —

Framework.
(d) taking steps to cease any University Digital Resource operation or activity that —
i. risks causing a major Cyber Security incident; or
ii. constitute unauthorised non-compliance with requirements of the Cyber Security
Framework.

Source ID: N067
Document title: Information Privacy Policy (pdf, 220 KB)
Printed bundle page: 343
Line numbers: L002–L014
Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
4.5 Monitoring and Continual Improvement
(a) A Privacy Impact Assessment (PIA) to identify privacy risks and measures to mitigate them must
be undertaken by the University where a new process or system involving personal information is
implemented or existing process changed that could reasonably have a significant impact on the
privacy of individuals.
(b) The University monitors, measures, analyses and evaluates information privacy compliance
performance, controls and processes to ensure they are and remain appropriate and effective.
(c) Significant risks, compliance matters and breaches are reported to relevant University committees
and external supervisory agencies where required in accordance with the University’s Information
Breach Policy.
(d) The University must continuously improve its information privacy practices in response to
monitoring outcomes, including results of reviews, audits, incidents, breaches, reported concerns,
and changes to legal or regulatory obligations.

(b) allocating sufficient resources and support for managing Cyber Security risks;
(c) suspending access to University Digital Resources by members of the University Community
where such access —
i. risks causing a major Cyber Security incident; or
ii. constitute unauthorised non-compliance with requirements of the Cyber Security
Framework.
(d) taking steps to cease any University Digital Resource operation or activity that —
i. risks causing a major Cyber Security incident; or
ii. constitute unauthorised non-compliance with requirements of the Cyber Security
Framework.

Case note:
UWA Information Privacy Policy N067 and Cyber Security Policy N072 regulate purpose-limited collection, Privacy Collection Notices, security, access/correction, and lawful disposal of student data. Although course-scoped AI memory is not named explicitly and the linked Information Retention Policy is absent from the bundle, existing privacy and retention duties address the critical risks. Note that Protocol v1.1 critical-risk criteria were adopted after preliminary review.

Evidence type: Exact quotation
Exact quotation:
(a) The University must only use or disclose Personal Information for the purpose for which it was
collected (the primary purpose), unless the individual would reasonably expect the use or
disclosure for a secondary purpose, and:
i. for Personal Information, the secondary purpose is related to the primary purpose; or
ii. for Sensitive Information, the secondary purpose is directly related to the primary purpose.
(b) Before Personal Information is used or disclosed for a secondary purpose that use or disclosure
must be documented.

4.4 Automated Decision Making (ADM)
When the University employs an ADM process involving the use of Personal Information that is used in
making Significant Decisions about individuals, the University must:
(a) Conduct and document an impact assessment that considers how to minimise the risk of harm,
bias and discrimination;
(b) Notify individuals that it is using ADM and, on request, provide information about how the ADM
process works; and
(c) Provide a process through which individuals can request human involvement in the decision.

5.1 Personal Information Requests
(a) Individuals have the right to access Personal Information held about them by the University and to
request correction of that information where it is inaccurate, incomplete or out-of-date.
(b) Requests for access to or correction of Personal Information must be submitted to the relevant
University function responsible for that information such as Student Central (for students) or People
and Culture (for staff) or to the UWA Privacy Office (privacy@uwa.edu.au).
(c) The University must take reasonable steps to provide access to, and correct, Personal Information
in a timely manner, subject to any legal restrictions or exceptions.
(d) Where the University refuses access to, or correction of, Personal Information, it must provide
reasons for the decision and information about available avenues for review or complaint.

5.2 Privacy Complaints
(a) The University will investigate and take seriously complaints made by individuals relating to the
University’s handling and management of Personal Information, including disclosure of their
information, where that is contrary to this Policy, or an interference with their privacy in accordance
with the PRIS Act.
(b) Complaints must be submitted in writing to the UWA Privacy Office (privacy@uwa.edu.au) and
include sufficient details of the Personal Information concerned, and the conduct alleged to be
inconsistent with this Policy or constitutes an interference with privacy.
(c) Privacy Complaints made to the UWA Privacy Office under 5.1(b) above will be dealt with as soon
as practical but where possible within 45 University working days.

4.4 Automated Decision Making (ADM)
When the University employs an ADM process involving the use of Personal Information that is used in
making Significant Decisions about individuals, the University must:
(a) Conduct and document an impact assessment that considers how to minimise the risk of harm,
bias and discrimination;
(b) Notify individuals that it is using ADM and, on request, provide information about how the ADM
process works; and
(c) Provide a process through which individuals can request human involvement in the decision.

4.5 Monitoring and Continual Improvement
(a) A Privacy Impact Assessment (PIA) to identify privacy risks and measures to mitigate them must
be undertaken by the University where a new process or system involving personal information is
implemented or existing process changed that could reasonably have a significant impact on the
privacy of individuals.
(b) The University monitors, measures, analyses and evaluates information privacy compliance
performance, controls and processes to ensure they are and remain appropriate and effective.
(c) Significant risks, compliance matters and breaches are reported to relevant University committees
and external supervisory agencies where required in accordance with the University’s Information
Breach Policy.
(d) The University must continuously improve its information privacy practices in response to
monitoring outcomes, including results of reviews, audits, incidents, breaches, reported concerns,
and changes to legal or regulatory obligations.

Brief explanation of which decision the route reviews:
Students may request access/correction under N067 5.1, human involvement in ADM under N067 4.4(c), privacy complaint review under N067 5.2, or academic decision review under N047.

Critical gap: Yes
Relevant pre-agreed critical risk:
Informed purpose and cross-course use, accountable lifecycle ownership, access/correction and contestability of consequential profiling.
Protocol v1.1 was adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15; it was not a criterion agreed before the original coding.
Specific gap and supporting evidence/search record:
Under Protocol v1.1 (adopted after preliminary review on 2026-11-15), although N067 4.2, 4.4, and 5.1 cover purpose limits, University ADM, and access/correction, UWA lacks explicit accountable lifecycle ownership, instructor visibility, and contestability rules for cross-course AI learner profiles influencing learning, assessment, and advice.

5.2 Privacy Complaints
(a) The University will investigate and take seriously complaints made by individuals relating to the
University’s handling and management of Personal Information, including disclosure of their

inconsistent with this Policy or constitutes an interference with privacy.
(c) Privacy Complaints made to the UWA Privacy Office under 5.1(b) above will be dealt with as soon
as practical but where possible within 45 University working days.

4.5 Monitoring and Continual Improvement
(a) A Privacy Impact Assessment (PIA) to identify privacy risks and measures to mitigate them must
be undertaken by the University where a new process or system involving personal information is
implemented or existing process changed that could reasonably have a significant impact on the
privacy of individuals.
(b) The University monitors, measures, analyses and evaluates information privacy compliance
performance, controls and processes to ensure they are and remain appropriate and effective.
(c) Significant risks, compliance matters and breaches are reported to relevant University committees
and external supervisory agencies where required in accordance with the University’s Information
Breach Policy.
(d) The University must continuously improve its information privacy practices in response to
monitoring outcomes, including results of reviews, audits, incidents, breaches, reported concerns,
and changes to legal or regulatory obligations.
